=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N) and an integer X.
Print one triple of integers (i, j, k) satisfying all of the following conditions. If no such triple exists, report that fact.

- 1 \leq i \lt j \lt k \leq N
- A_i + A_j + A_k = X

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N X
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

If there exists an integer triple (i, j, k) satisfying the conditions, print one in the following format. If there are multiple solutions, you may print any of them.
i j k

If no such triple exists, print -1.

Constraints


- 3 \leq N \leq 10^6
- 1 \leq X \leq 10^6
- 1 \leq A_i \leq X
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 16
1 8 5 10 13

Sample Output 1

1 3 4

The triple (i, j, k) = (1, 3, 4) satisfies 1 \leq i \lt j \lt k \leq N and A_i + A_j + A_k = 1 + 5 + 10 = 16 = X.

Sample Input 2

5 20
1 8 5 10 13

Sample Output 2

-1

Sample Input 3

10 100000
73766 47718 74148 49218 76721 31902 21994 18880 29598 98917

Sample Output 3

4 6 8

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's consider implementing NTT. Need handle counts and output indices. Let's detail.

Given freq array length X+1 (indices 0..X). Ai >=1, so freq[0]=0. Need find values a,b,c such that a+b+c=X and counts condition:
- If a,b,c all distinct: freq[a]>=1, freq[b]>=1, freq[c]>=1.
- If two equal: e.g., a=b != c: freq[a]>=2, freq[c]>=1.
- If all equal: freq[a]>=3.

We can first compute convolution of freq with itself to get ordered pair counts for each sum s: pair_count[s] = sum_{b=0}^s freq[b] * freq[s-b]. This counts ordered pairs (b,c) with values b,c, including b=c using same occurrence? Actually if b=c, freq[b]^2 counts ordered pairs of two occurrences, requiring two occurrences. Good. For b != c, freq[b]*freq[c] counts ordered pairs (b,c) and (c,b). If freq[b]=freq[c]=1, pair_count[s]=2 for that unordered pair. Existence if >0. For pair b=c, pair_count[2b] = freq[b]^2, so if freq[b]>=2 then >0; if freq[b]=1 then 1 (but uses same occurrence twice, not valid as two distinct elements). For pair sum T, if pair_count[T] > 0, does it guarantee two distinct indices? Only if the pair is not b=b with freq[b]=1. If freq[b]=1, pair_count[2b]=1 but invalid. So need adjust. For pair b=c, if freq[b]>=2 valid. If freq[b]=1 invalid. For b != c, pair_count includes ordered pairs, valid if both freq>=1. So pair_count[T] > 0 may be false positive for T=2b with freq[b]=1. Need handle when using pair as two elements.

Then for each a, need pair sum T = X-a. We need pair_count[T] > 0 and pair valid not using the same occurrence as a. Cases:
- If pair values b,c are both != a: valid if pair valid.
- If one pair value equals a: then need pair uses an occurrence of a distinct from the chosen a, so freq[a] >= 2 for that pair? More generally if b=a, c != a, pair valid if freq[a]>=2 and freq[c]>=1. If b=c=a, need freq[a]>=3.
- If b=c != a, pair valid if freq[b]>=2.
- If b=c=a, pair valid if freq[a]>=3.

Could implement NTT with optimizations. Let's estimate NTT time in Python. Length 2^21, mod 998244353. A typical iterative NTT:
```
def ntt(a, invert):
    n=len(a)
    h=n.bit_length()-1
    if not invert:
        length=1
        while length<n:
            wlen=pow(root,(mod-1)//(length*2),mod)
            for i in range(0,n,length*2):
                w=1
                for j in range(i,i+length):
                    u=a[j]; v=a[j+length]*w%mod
                    a[j]=(u+v)%mod
                    a[j+length]=(u-v)%mod
                    w=w*wlen%mod
            length*=2
    else: ...
```

Thus invalid pairs possible:
1. Pair containing a with freq[a]=1 (distinct a,c): contributes 2 bits.
2. Square pair {d,d} with freq[d]=1 (d != a): contributes 1 bit.
3. Square pair {a,a} with freq[a] <=2: contributes 1 bit.
All other pairs valid.

Therefore if mask has any bit not belonging to these invalid pairs, valid. The invalid pairs are at most two unordered pairs: the a-containing pair and the square pair (which may be same if a = T/2). So at most 3 bits (if a-containing distinct and square distinct). Thus if we iterate over set bits in mask, after checking at most 3 bits, either find valid or know none. Great! So per a, we only need inspect up to 3 bits. But we need an efficient way to iterate set bits and skip pairs.

We can implement:
```
mask = P & (C >> a)
while mask:
    lb = mask & -mask
    b = lb.bit_length() - 1
    c = X - a - b
    if 1 <= c <= X and freq[c] > 0:  # should hold
        # check counts
        if valid(a,b,c):
            output
    mask ^= lb  # remove this b
```

Let's verify bitset query with counts and indices.

But we can avoid storing all positions by storing `pos` as list of lists? 1e6 empty lists ~56MB plus positions ints, maybe okay but list overhead high. Use arrays.

Let's implement position storage efficiently:
```
first = [0]*(X+1)
second = [0]*(X+1)
third = [0]*(X+1)
cnt = [0]*(X+1)  # frequency
for idx, val in enumerate(A, 1):
    c = cnt[val]
    if c == 0: first[val] = idx
    elif c == 1: second[val] = idx
    elif c == 2: third[val] = idx
    cnt[val] = c + 1
```
Need cnt for counts. We can use `cnt` list. For positions, if count >3, we don't need store more. But if count is 3 and another occurrence, ignore. We only need up to 3 positions. Good.

But we also need freq for bitset; cnt is freq.

Build bytearray for P:
```
nbytes = (X + 1 + 7) // 8
ba = bytearray(nbytes)
for v in range(1, X+1):
    if cnt[v]:
        ba[v >> 3] |= 1 << (v & 7)
P = int.from_bytes(ba, 'little')
```
Loop over X=1e6 okay. But we also need C. We can build C bytearray similarly:
```
cb = bytearray(nbytes)
for v in range(1, X+1):
    if cnt[v]:
        cb[(X - v) >> 3] |= 1 << ((X - v) & 7)
C = int.from_bytes(cb, 'little')
```
This loops over X twice. Fine.

Can we build C from P more directly? Bytearray approach okay.

Need compute max_pair_sum and min_pair_sum:
- max_val = max v with cnt[v]>0.
- second_max: if cnt[max_val] >= 2, second_max = max_val; else find next v < max_val with cnt[v]>0.
- min_val, second_min similarly: if cnt[min_val]>=2, second_min=min_val; else next > min_val.
- max_pair_sum = max_val + second_max
- min_pair_sum = min_val + second_min
If N>=3, these exist.

Now loop over small a:
```
limit = X // 3
for a in range(1, limit + 1):
    if cnt[a] == 0: continue
    T = X - a
    if T > max_pair_sum or T < min_pair_sum:
        continue
    mask = P & (C >> a)
    while mask:
        lb = mask & -mask
        b = lb.bit_length() - 1
        mask ^= lb
        c = T - b
        if c < 1 or c > X or cnt[c] == 0:
            continue  # should not happen
        # Check counts and output
```
Need ensure b maybe 0? Values >=1, P bit 0 zero, so b>=1. c>=1.

Counts check:
```
if a == b == c:
    if cnt[a] < 3: continue
elif a == b or a == c:
    if cnt[a] < 2: continue
elif b == c:
    if cnt[b] < 2: continue
else:
    # all distinct, cnt all >=1 by construction
    pass
```
But note if a==b and cnt[a]>=2, valid. If a==c similarly. If b==c and cnt[b]>=2. If a==b==c, cnt>=3.

Then output indices. Need choose positions:
- If a==b==c: need three positions of value a: first[a], second[a], third[a] (cnt>=3).
- If a==b != c: need two positions of a and one of c. Since cnt[a]>=2, positions first[a], second[a] for a and first[c] for c. But output indices must be sorted i<j<k. We can collect positions and sort.
- If a==c != b: need two positions of a and one of b.
- If b==c != a: need one position of a and two of b.
- All distinct: first[a], first[b], first[c].
Then sort the three indices and print.

But wait our query chooses `a` as the small value. If a==b and cnt[a]>=2, we need two positions of a. We have first and second. If a==b==c, need first, second, third. Good.

Need ensure that the pair (b,c) found by mask is valid as two distinct indices. Our counts check covers:
- If b==c, cnt[b]>=2. If a==b==c, cnt[a]>=3.
- If b!=c, each has cnt>=1; if a equals one of them, cnt[a]>=2. Good.

But what if mask finds b and c such that b+c=T, but P_b and P_c are present. If b==c and cnt[b]=1, mask has bit b set because P_b and C_{a+b}? Let's verify. For b=c, C_{a+b} = P_{X-a-b} = P_b. Since P_b=1, mask includes b even if cnt[b]=1. Our counts check will reject if b==c and cnt[b]<2. Good. If there is another valid pair, mask may have more bits; we iterate. If only invalid square, we continue to next a. Good.

Potential issue: `mask = P & (C >> a)` includes b values where P_b and P_{T-b}. But if b = T-b (square), it includes b once. If b != T-b, it includes both b and c. Our loop over mask bits will check both b and c. For a distinct invalid pair containing a, it will check b=a and b=c. Both fail if cnt[a]=1. Then continue. If there is another valid pair, mask has other bits; we'll find. At most 3 bits as argued, but our while loop could in theory iterate over many bits if many pairs. But if many pairs, we'll find valid quickly. However, consider a case where many pairs all invalid? We argued only two invalid unordered pairs. Let's prove to ensure no performance issue:
For fixed a, T=X-a. Any unordered pair {u,v} with u+v=T.
- If u != v, mask has bits u and v.
- If u=v, mask has bit u.
Validity with third a:
  * If a not in {u,v}: valid as long as u!=v or cnt[u]>=2. If u=v and cnt[u]=1, invalid. Only one square pair.
  * If a in {u,v}: If u=v=a, need cnt[a]>=3; invalid if cnt[a]<=2. If u=a, v!=a, need cnt[a]>=2; invalid if cnt[a]=1. Only one pair contains a (because for fixed T, the partner of a is unique). Thus invalid pairs are at most the pair containing a and the square pair (which may coincide). So at most 3 mask bits. Therefore while loop per a at most 3 iterations. Good. So per a O(1) bit extractions.

Now, can we skip `a` values where cnt[a]=0; yes.

What about iterating only a <= X//3. Suppose triple has small value a but a appears in pair and cnt[a]=1? If triple is (a,b,c) with a small and a equals b? Then triple uses a twice, so cnt[a]>=2. If a equals c similarly. If all distinct, pair doesn't use a. So if triple exists, our query with that small a will find a valid pair. Good.

But what if the small value in the triple is not unique? There may be multiple small values; any works. We iterate all.

Now, complexity of building C bytearray: We loop over v 1..X and check cnt. Fine. But we also need list of small values? We can just loop range(1, limit+1) and check cnt. That's 333k iterations. Good.

Potential issue: `C >> a` where a up to X//3. If a is large, shift result has length X-a bits. `P & (C >> a)` works. But if a > X? no.

Let's test with sample.

Sample1: X=16, A=[1,8,5,10,13].
limit=5. small values: 1,5.
a=1, T=15. P bits 1,5,8,10,13. C bit y=P_{16-y}. C>>1. mask includes b=5? P5=1, C_{6}=P10=1, yes. b=5, c=10. a=1 distinct, valid. Output positions first[1]=1, first[5]=3, first[10]=4 sorted -> 1 3 4. Good.

Sample2 X=20. limit=6. small values:1,5? A same. a=1, T=19. Need pair sum 19: 8+11 no, 5+14 no, 10+9 no, 13+6 no. mask zero. a=5, T=15. Pair sum 15: 1+14 no, 5+10? b=5,c=10, a=5, cnt5=1 invalid; mask includes b=5 and b=10? P5=1, C_{10}=P5? Wait a=5, T=15. mask = P & (C>>5). For b=5, C_{10}=P_{20-5-5}=P10=1, so bit5 set. For b=10, C_{15}=P5=1, bit10 set. Both invalid because a=5 cnt1. No other pair. Continue. No output -> -1. Good.

Now, let's implement `ints`:
```
def ints():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    num = 0
    in_num = False
    for b in data:
        if 48 <= b <= 57:
            num = num * 10 + (b - 48)
            in_num = True
        elif in_num:
            yield num
            num = 0
            in_num = False
    if in_num:
        yield num
```
This yields all ints. Good.
